---
name: code-review
description: Review the changes since a fixed point (commit, branch, tag, or merge-base) along two axes — Standards (does the code follow this repo's documented coding standards?) and Spec (does the code match what the originating issue/PRD asked for?). Runs both reviews in parallel sub-agents and reports them side by side. Use when the user wants to review a branch, a PR, work-in-progress changes, or asks to "review since X".
---

Two-axis review of the diff between `HEAD` and a fixed point the user supplies:

- **Standards** — does the code conform to this repo's documented coding standards?
- **Spec** — does the code faithfully implement the originating issue / PRD / spec?

Both axes run as **parallel sub-agents** so they don't pollute each other's context, then this skill aggregates their findings.

The issue tracker should have been provided to you — run `/setup-matt-pocock-skills` if `docs/agents/issue-tracker.md` is missing.

## Process

### 1. Pin the fixed point

Whatever the user said is the fixed point — a commit SHA, branch name, tag, `main`, `HEAD~5`, etc. If they didn't specify one, ask for it.

Capture the diff command once: `git diff <fixed-point>...HEAD` (three-dot, so the comparison is against the merge-base). Also note the list of commits via `git log <fixed-point>..HEAD --oneline`.

Before going further, confirm the fixed point resolves (`git rev-parse <fixed-point>`) and the diff is non-empty. A bad ref or empty diff should fail here — not inside two parallel sub-agents.

### 2. Identify the spec source

Look for the originating spec, in this order:

1. Issue references in the commit messages (`#123`, `Closes #45`, GitLab `!67`, etc.) — fetch via the workflow in `docs/agents/issue-tracker.md`.
2. A path the user passed as an argument.
3. A PRD/spec file under `docs/`, `specs/`, or `.scratch/` matching the branch name or feature.
4. If nothing is found, ask the user where the spec is. If they say there isn't one, the **Spec** sub-agent will skip and report "no spec available".

### 3. Identify the standards sources

Anything in the repo that documents how code should be written, such as `CODING_STANDARDS.md` or `CONTRIBUTING.md`.

On top of whatever the repo documents, the Standards axis always carries the **smell baseline** below — a fixed set of Fowler code smells (_Refactoring_, ch.3) that applies even when a repo documents nothing. Two rules bind it:

- **The repo overrides.** A documented repo standard always wins; where it endorses something the baseline would flag, suppress the smell.
- **Always a judgement call.** Each smell is a labelled heuristic ("possible Feature Envy"), never a hard violation — and, like any standard here, skip anything tooling already enforces.

Each smell reads *what it is* → *how to fix*; match it against the diff:

- **Mysterious Name** — a function, variable, or type whose name doesn't reveal what it does or holds. → rename it; if no honest name comes, the design's murky.
- **Duplicated Code** — the same logic shape appears in more than one hunk or file in the change. → extract the shared shape, call it from both.
- **Feature Envy** — a method that reaches into another object's data more than its own. → move the method onto the data it envies.
- **Data Clumps** — the same few fields or params keep travelling together (a type wanting to be born). → bundle them into one type, pass that.
- **Primitive Obsession** — a primitive or string standing in for a domain concept that deserves its own type. → give the concept its own small type.
- **Repeated Switches** — the same `switch`/`if`-cascade on the same type recurs across the change. → replace with polymorphism, or one map both sites share.
- **Shotgun Surgery** — one logical change forces scattered edits across many files in the diff. → gather what changes together into one module.
- **Divergent Change** — one file or module is edited for several unrelated reasons. → split so each module changes for one reason.
- **Speculative Generality** — abstraction, parameters, or hooks added for needs the spec doesn't have. → delete it; inline back until a real need shows.
- **Message Chains** — long `a.b().c().d()` navigation the caller shouldn't depend on. → hide the walk behind one method on the first object.
- **Middle Man** — a class or function that mostly just delegates onward. → cut it, call the real target direct.
- **Refused Bequest** — a subclass or implementer that ignores or overrides most of what it inherits. → drop the inheritance, use composition.

### 4. Spawn both sub-agents in parallel

Send a single message with two `Agent` tool calls. Use the `general-purpose` subagent for both.

**Standards sub-agent prompt** — include:

- The full diff command and commit list.
- The list of standards-source files you found in step 3, **plus the smell baseline from step 3** pasted in full — the sub-agent has no other access to it.
- The brief: "Report — per file/hunk where relevant — (a) every place the diff violates a documented standard: cite the standard (file + the rule); and (b) any baseline smell you spot: name it and quote the hunk. Distinguish hard violations from judgement calls — documented-standard breaches can be hard, but baseline smells are always judgement calls, and a documented repo standard overrides the baseline. Skip anything tooling enforces. Under 400 words."

**Spec sub-agent prompt** — include:

- The diff command and commit list.
- The path or fetched contents of the spec.
- The brief: "Report: (a) requirements the spec asked for that are missing or partial; (b) behaviour in the diff that wasn't asked for (scope creep); (c) requirements that look implemented but where the implementation looks wrong. Quote the spec line for each finding. Under 400 words."

If the spec is missing, skip the Spec sub-agent and note this in the final report.

### 5. Aggregate

Present the two reports under `## Standards` and `## Spec` headings, verbatim or lightly cleaned. Do **not** merge or rerank findings — the two axes are deliberately separate (see _Why two axes_).

End with a one-line summary: total findings per axis, and the worst issue _within each axis_ (if any). Don't pick a single winner across axes — that's the reranking the separation exists to prevent.

## Why two axes

A change can pass one axis and fail the other:

- Code that follows every standard but implements the wrong thing → **Standards pass, Spec fail.**
- Code that does exactly what the issue asked but breaks the project's conventions → **Spec pass, Standards fail.**

Reporting them separately stops one axis from masking the other.
---
name: research
description: Investigate a question against high-trust primary sources and capture the findings as a Markdown file in the repo. Use when the user wants a topic researched, docs or API facts gathered, or reading legwork delegated to a background agent.
---

Spin up a **background agent** to do the research, so you keep working while it reads.

Its job:

1. Investigate the question against **primary sources** — official docs, source code, specs, first-party APIs — not a secondary write-up of them. Follow every claim back to the source that owns it.
2. Write the findings to a single Markdown file, citing each claim's source.
3. Save it where the repo already keeps such notes; match the existing convention, and if there is none, put it somewhere sensible and say where.

# Response to the 2026-09-19 external review (3/10)

All substantive issues are addressed in a new revision; every statistical
change is validated offline (no new paid model calls). Below we answer the
four questions posed in the review verbatim.

## Q1. Which output is the calibrated "C-comp"? Explain the clone arm result.

The scientific C-comp state is now
`E1_C-rank_E2_C-comp.stable_complementarity.clone_calibrated` produced by
`experiment/diagnostics/ccomp_v3.py`. The old repeat-averaged block is
renamed `descriptive_plugin` and can never determine a state.

Rules separating descriptive from scientific output:

1. The plug-in max of noisy per-task estimates is always printed under
   `descriptive_plugin.H_hat` with an explicit upward-bias warning.
2. The scientific estimand is the cross-fitted frozen-selection gain
   `G`: in each of three folds, two repeats freeze BOTH the per-task
   member mapping h*(x) AND the single best fixed member h_fix; the held-
   out repeat scores the frozen mapping against that fixed member
   (never bare).
3. The decision statistic is `D = G_real − G_clone`, with an exact
   two-arm randomization p-value (member labels permuted independently
   within each task AND repeat in both arms) and a paired task bootstrap.
4. A SUPPORTED state requires the coverage gate, randomization p < .05,
   bootstrap lower bound > 0, D > 1pp, and a positive G_real lower bound.

On the sealed data: descriptive plug-in = 2.54pp real / 2.67pp clone
(known biased; the clone value is the reviewer's exact point made
empirically); G_real = 0.29pp [−0.57, 1.24]; G_clone = 0.70pp;
D = 0.10pp [−1.36, 1.66], p = .73; state INSUFFICIENT_EVIDENCE
(coverage gate 12.5% > 10%).

## Q2. A8.6 output under the bare=0.5 / others=0.9 example.

That statistic cannot distinguish global dominance from complementarity:
its expectation is ≈ 0.5·(1−0.1^8)·0.4 ≈ 0.20. We encode the example as a
regression test (`test_w2_global_dominance_no_support`) and relabel A8.6
(`E2_legacy_vs_bare_A86`, field `estimand_status`) as a SECONDARY /
DESCRIPTIVE post-discovery advantage over bare, never a C-comp test.

The primary v3 estimand compares the frozen selector with the best FIXED
member. Under the 0.5/0.9 configuration the frozen best-fixed member is
one of the 0.9 members on discovery folds and G_real ≤ 0 in population;
the randomization test does not support (verified FPR = 0 over repeated
draws). The two-arm subtraction makes this exact at equal ability and
conservative throughout the dominance null family.

## Q3. "Equal budget" — precise definition and the spend discrepancy.

Three comparisons are now explicitly separated in §6.2 and
Appendix C:

- Equal-slot collection (what was run): 9 members/slots × 3 repeats each.
- Common per-task execution budget (the cost-controlled claim): new
  offline analysis `experiment/revision/wp1r_budget.py` compares the
  dev-ranked panel portfolio with b ∈ {3,…,27} of the 27 same-code
  executions. Oracle coverage is tied (−0.25 to +0.37pp at all b);
  majority-vote aggregation favors same-code by 6.1–27.3pp.
- Realized spend: reported, not matched — real 22,192 logical calls
  (54.17M tokens, 54.5/task) vs clone 10,800 calls (14.51M tokens,
  27/task), a 2.05× call and 3.73× token ratio. No cost-matched claim is
  made; the common-budget table carries the cost-controlled conclusion.

## Q4. Error rates and power by M / T / R with missingness and correlations.

New known-truth simulation package:
`experiment/diagnostics/{calibration_sim.py,calibration_stats.py,
calibration_run.py}`, rendered by `calibration_render.py` into main-text
Table 5 and documented in Appendix G. Regimes: iid equal ability
(constant and heterogeneous-difficulty); global dominance (clean,
reviewer 0.5/0.9, heterogeneous); crossover alternatives of four
strengths; MCAR 5% and hardness-related MNAR 3%; grid
M ∈ {3,9,20}, T ∈ {100,400,1000}, R ∈ {3,5,10}; 300 datasets per core
cell (150 in sweeps), 500 randomizations per test. Reported per cell:
false-support rate (nulls), power (alternatives), abstention rate, CI
coverage, mean estimate, for the legacy plug-in, one-split frozen gain,
repeat-cross-fitted gain, and the v3 clone-calibrated decision.

Headline results (filled in from the run artifact):
- plug-in false-support ≈ 1.00 on EVERY null at every grid point;
- v3 false-support ≤ ~0.02 on nulls including both reviewer
  counterexamples (Wilson 95% intervals in the JSON);
- v3 power at R=3 is limited to large interactions and grows with R
  (moderate interactions need R≈10, strong ones R=20) — reported as an
  abstention property, not hidden;
- missingness at the frozen levels does not move null FPR;
- sticky within-task latent state shared across repeats is
  false-support-free when the clone arm shares the same structure
  (matched FPR ≤ .02); panel-only sticky state is genuine realized
  interaction (H≈.18) and is not an H=0 DGP.

The size argument is structural: under the global-dominance cone the
frozen mapping routes to the dominant member or misroutes away from it,
so the population gain θ* ≤ 0; the equal-ability randomization is exact
at that boundary and conservative throughout the cone. The required
exchangeability condition is stated: the validation repeat is a fresh
execution conditionally independent of discovery given (member, task)
— cache/session/RNG state shared across repeats but absent in deployment
would violate it (a repeat-integrity gate is listed as future work).

## Other points

- C-rank: per-pair bootstrap replaced by a shared bootstrap max-T
  simultaneous interval over all 36 pairs; population state text now says
  "at least one pair", not "the ranking is stable".
- p = .9980: text states precisely that it is an in-arm clone-
  permutation p for the legacy vs-bare statistic with different task sets
  (350/383/342); the v3 primary p is .7309.
- Identical-matrix gate: equality now triggers provenance verification
  (distinct execution IDs) rather than auto-rejection; near-deterministic
  programs can legitimately reproduce matrices, and repeated results are
  not evidence of copied execution.
- Jensen statement narrowed to the per-task term with a two-member/
  two-task downward-bias counterexample (true H 0.40, expected plug-in
  ~0.34); what is claimed is the same-code false-positive mechanism,
  demonstrated exactly, not a general sign theorem for the difference.
- Scope: the real-data conclusion is stated only for this panel, these
  previously analyzed tasks, these execution/missingness conditions; no
  generalization to harnesses in general; the within-task repeat-mean
  imputation is presented as a sensitivity with its MAR assumption and the
  MNAR abstention boundary.

## Verification

- `python -m pytest experiment/diagnostics -q` — 54 tests pass,
  including the two reviewer counterexamples, the crossover positive
  control, clone-required abstention, and missingness gates.
- WP-1R analysis, renderer, budget curves, and LaTeX macros regenerated
  from the sealed ledgers; no hand-copied numbers.
